And I guess I would just argue that all of us eventually figure out that you want the long-term things and the things that make you personally better off, and then after that you can do the things that make all of society better off, because if you just obsess on yourself, you're not going to be happy.

哲学

Philosophy

埃里克：这联想大概有点夸张，但感觉你是在写自己的《沉思录》。你知道的，是公开的、写给自己的笔记，而且你还在不停改。

Eric: Well, it's probably a grandiose association, but it feels like you're writing your Meditations. You know, they're notes to self that are public that you're iterating on.

纳瓦尔：《沉思录》更真，因为他本来没打算把日记拿去出版。

Naval: Well, Meditations was more authentic because he didn't mean for his journal to be published.

埃里克：是的。所以，对，这里就有观察者效应。

Eric: Yes. So, yeah, you have the observer effect.

纳瓦尔：是，我的肯定没那么真。我尽量在公开的同时保持真实，对吧？但只要公开，就已经掺进了一定程度的不真实。我真正佩服的是叔本华：他活着的时候写了那么多刺耳的真话，没人喜欢他，但他知道自己说的是真的，所以不在乎。如今他身后极负盛名——至少应该如此。他是我读过的西方哲学家里面最伟大的一位。也许哪天我会写下我真正的书，身后才出版——我确实活在这个世界里，如今世界又高度互联，我还有孩子，等等。到那时候，我才会把那些刺耳的真话全说出来。

Naval: Yes, mine is less authentic for sure. I try to keep it as authentic as I can while being public, right? Just being public has some level of inauthenticity baked into it. The guy I really admire, Schopenhauer, 'cause he wrote so much harsh truth while he was alive and nobody liked him, but he knew he was telling the truth, so he didn't care. And now he's posthumously incredibly famous, or should be anyway. I think he's the greatest of the Western philosophers that I've encountered. Maybe I'll write my real book and it gets published posthumously 'cause I do live in this world and now the world is super connected and I have kids and all that. But that's when I tell all the harsh truths.

埃里克：对。你给过一个正在找目标、找意义、为此不快乐的人一条建议：上帝、孩子，或者使命。

Eric: Yeah. One of the pieces of advice you gave somebody who was presently unhappy about finding purpose, finding meaning was God, kids, or mission.

纳瓦尔：对，没错。上帝、孩子，或者使命。这三样至少得有一样。

Naval: Yeah, that's right. Yeah, God, kids, or mission. You've got to find one of the three at least.

埃里克：我好奇这三样在你自己身上分别是怎么分布的。

Eric: I was curious how those broke down for you personally.

纳瓦尔：我三样都有。我的「上帝」是很私人的，你知道，我甚至说不清楚。我都不确定自己会不会用「上帝」这个词，但它在那儿。不管这到底是什么，我和它有自己的关系。孩子，肯定有。我爱他们，把这份爱往外扩——就是家人。家人太好了。跟家人亲近，爱家人，照顾他们。没有替代品。而且年纪越大，越要把「家人」的定义放宽。使命也有。对，我在做一个产品，在跟你录这个东西，我在试着弄清自己最擅长什么，并在那上面实现自己。

Naval: I got all three. Well, my God is personal, you know. I can't even articulate it. I'm not even sure I use the word God, but it's there. I have my own relationship with whatever this is. Kids, definitely have them. Love them. Broaden that. Just family. Family is great. Be close to family. Love your family. Take care of them. There's no substitutes. And expand your definition of family as you get older. And mission. Yeah. I mean, I have a product that I'm working on. I'm recording this thing with you. I'm trying to self-actualize as to what I do best.

埃里克：所以这个使命很窄，就是你眼下在做的事，不是那种人类层面、文明层面的宏大使命。

Eric: So, the mission is very narrow to what you're working on now. It's not a grander human-level, civilization-level.

纳瓦尔：我想开悟。可如果我自己没把这些深奥的问题一路想到底，我觉得就没资格拿它们去教训别人。所以某种程度上，我一直没写书，部分原因是觉得那可能是一种欺瞒。你知道，还有一部分纯粹是我懒。所以这是个好借口，但也可能真是欺瞒。

Naval: I'd like to be enlightened. I don't feel like I have a right to lecture people on these esoteric topics without myself having explored them all the way through. So in some level part of the reason why I haven't written a book is because I feel like it might be fraudulent. You know part of I'm just lazy. So it's a good excuse but it might be fraudulent.

埃里克：内省会过量吗？

Eric: Can you overdose on introspection?

纳瓦尔：我觉得那是另一回事。内省——如果你是在找真相，在观察自己、提高自我觉察——我不认为会过量。还没等你过量，你就腻了，或者走神了。但如果你是在反刍，脑子里翻来覆去「这不该发生」「那样不对」，陷进抑郁的人常有的那种思维循环，那你确实会做过头。所以要看你在想什么。观察自己，和沉溺于自己、沉溺于某些问题，是两回事——那些问题里有些是真问题，但多数是想出来的。比如我有一个有争议的看法——有人不爱听——就是：我在读一位哲学家的时候，刚发了条推文说：越是在乎这话是谁说的，它其实越不重要。我认为这是真的。真理就是真理，不管它出自谁口。但与此同时，还有「怎么过一生」这类哲学，它们可不是一句两句的漂亮话，而是一个人的整套哲学，对吧？如今我不读书，我读作者；再进一步，我不读作者，我读哲学家，对吧？所以我读多伊奇，读奥修，读克里希那穆提，读叔本华；因为喜欢他们，我就把他们写的一切都读了。既然他们的一切还没读完，我为什么要去读别人？对吧？塔勒布的东西我也全读，对吧？但我确实会拿这些哲学家在人生里拿到的结果来评判他们。要是某人有一套很深的哲学，可他看上去并不快乐，也没得到自己想要的，还为此活得挺痛苦——那就像《老无所依》里那句台词：你的那些规矩把你带到了这儿，它们还有什么用？杀手是在主角出现在不该出现的地方时说这句话的，对吧？哲学本该把你带到一个好地方。所以有些当代的作者和名人——我就不点名了——基本是自杀收场。我就想：那套哲学到底有多厉害？我不是不同情你，但显然你按自己的哲学做的事，没起作用。也许那里头有某些真理可以摘出来，但我不会把整个身家投进你的哲学，也不会认可你那种结局糟糕的人生。

Naval: I think it would be a different thing. Introspection if you're trying to find truth, if you're like thinking like watching yourself and increasing your self-awareness. I don't think you can. I think you get bored of it or distracted before you overdose on it. But if you're ruminating, if you're basically like, oh, this shouldn't have happened and this was wrong, and you know, you're caught in that mind loop that depressed people have, then you can absolutely overdo it. So, it depends what you're thinking about. If you're observing yourself, that's different than if you're obsessing over yourself or you're obsessing over some problems, some of which can be real, but most of which are imagined. Like one of the controversial takes I have—people don't like this—but if I'm reading a philosopher, right, one tweet I just put was like the more it matters who said it, the less it actually matters. I think that is true. Like truth is truth regardless of who it comes from. But at the same time there are philosophies of how to live your life and these aren't just like simple statements, these are like this person's entire philosophy, right? And I don't read books these days, I read authors, and I don't read authors, I read philosophers, right? So I'll read Deutsch, I'll read Osho, I'll read Krishnamurti, I'll read Schopenhauer, and because I like them, I'll read everything by them. And until I'm done reading everything by them, why would I read anybody else? Right? I'll read everything by Taleb, right? But I do judge these philosophers on the outcomes they got in their lives. So if somebody has a deep philosophy but they seem like they were unhappy and they didn't get what they wanted. They were miserable about it, then you know it's like that line from No Country for Old Men: like what good are your rules if they led you here? The killer says that when the protagonist shows up in the wrong place, right? So it should lead you to a good place. So there are some modern writers of recent times and celebrities who I won't name but they basically kill themselves. So I'm like, how great was that philosophy? Like I'm sorry you killed yourself, but clearly whatever you did as your philosophy did not work. Now there might be individual truths in there I can pluck out, but I'm not going to invest deeply in your philosophy or ratify your life because it ended badly.

埃里克：你有条推特很棒：「真实的真理都是异端。它们不能被说出来，只能被发现、被低声传述，也许还能被读到。」

Eric: You've got a great tweet. The real truths are heresies. They cannot be spoken, only discovered, whispered, and perhaps read.

纳瓦尔：对。所以它是我最幽默的一本书。

Naval: That's right. So it's my most humorous book.

埃里克：可为什么会这样？为什么有那么多力量——哦，社会会把你私刑处死？为什么反对真理的力量这么多？

Eric: Why is that though? Like why are there so many—oh, society will lynch you? Why are there so many forces like working against truth?

纳瓦尔：因为社会得维持在一起。群体必须有共识。要有共识，就得有一套共享的信念——它们是假的，却能让大家相处得更容易。其中一条就是「人人平等」，对吧？在圣灵的意义上人人平等；在意识的意义上人人平等；在「普遍解释者」的意义上人人平等。可在几乎任何其他意义上，人并不平等，对吧？你多努力、你对社会或对自己有多大贡献、你的产品有多好、你能跳多高、你能打多重、你能不能胜任这份工作或那份工作——都不平等，对吧？但这是我们必须共有的一则虚构，否则没法相处。没有这则共同虚构，我们就会开始互相争斗。所以有些假话，是出于很好的理由才在那里的：它们防止内斗，让群体得以共存。只是它们并不真。

Naval: Because society has to stay together. Groups have to have consensus. And to have consensus you have to have a shared set of beliefs that are false but make it easier to get along. And like one of those is everyone's equal, right? Everyone's equal in the Holy Ghost sense. Everyone's equal in this consciousness sense. Everyone's equal in the universal-explainer sense. Everyone is not equal in almost any other sense, right? In terms of like how hard you work, how good are you doing for society or for yourself, how good is your product, how high can you jump, how hard can you hit, you know, are you qualified for this job or that job? Not equal, right? But that is a shared fiction that we have to have to get along. If we don't have that shared fiction, we don't get along. We start fighting each other. So, some of these falsehoods are there for good reasons. They prevent warfare. They allow the group to coexist, but they're not true.

举个简单的例子，还有很多。男女关系里就常见，对吧？那整套「把妹达人」是怎么回事？无非是一群男人互相分享关于女人的、在公开场合不敢说的「真相」——这些说法对某一些女人似乎管用，让他们能约到会。比如这个领域里有一条：女人会被男人身上象征社会地位的信号吸引。不一定是财富，尽管财富可以是这种信号；也不一定是体格，甚至不一定是品格。虽然我会说，谈到婚姻，女人看重品格；但约会嘛，她们看的是社会地位的信号，对吧？甚至都不是社会地位本身，是它的信号，对吧？于是男人就得学着破解这些信号。这就催生出一种「把戏」现象，而它本身又是一套针对女人的虚假说法，对吧？反过来也一样：女人被教导要相信，男人是因为你的品格和个性才被你吸引。没错，后来他们也许会因此留下来，但最初的吸引是生理上的，对吧？这也是另一条不太能公开多谈的真相。这些还处在边缘，所以我可以稍微说两句。有些真相我还往下压得很深，根本不能碰。但我要告诉你：历史上最伟大的一些哲学家，正是靠说出刺耳的真话，才确立了自己讲真话的资格。也正因如此，他们会比其他大多数人被遗忘得更晚，依然有人读。这就是去读那些已经死去的人写下的、永恒而完满的书的价值所在——他们已经死了。他们要么把真话说了出来，你知道，要么身后出版，要么匿名出版，要么干脆不在乎。

So, I mean, that's a simple example. There are many. Like you see this a lot in male-female relationships, right? Like what is the whole pickup-artist thing, the pickup-artist society? That's all guys sharing truths about women that they're not allowed to say in public that seem to work on some number of women that allow them to go get dates. You know, like one of the truths in that domain is that women are attracted to signs of social status in men. It's not they're attracted to necessarily wealth, although wealth can be a sign of social status. They're not necessarily attracted to physicality or even character. Although I would argue for marriage, women are attracted to character, but you know, for dating, they're attracted to signs of social status in men. It's not even social status. It's signs of social status, right? Right? So then men have to learn how to hack signs of social status. That creates this game phenomenon which is its own falsehood going back at women. You know equivalently like women are taught to believe that like you know men are attracted to you for your character and your personality. Yeah they might stay with you for that later but the initial attraction is all physical, right? And so that's another truth you're not supposed to talk about too much. These are on the edge so I can talk about them a little bit. There are truths I just cannot talk about, them just way further down. But I would tell you that some of the greatest philosophers of all time, they establish their truth-telling capabilities by telling you the harsh truth. And because of that, like they'll be read long after most of the rest of them are forgotten. And that's part of the value of reading those permanent perfect books from dead people who were willing to tell the truth. They're dead. And they either—either the truth was told, you know, they published posthumously or they published anonymously or they just didn't care.

埃里克：有意思。有些真理会自我延续、自我收敛，对吧？它们让自己越来越显明；另一些则必须被主动遮掩。

Eric: It's interesting that some truths need to be—some truths are self-perpetuating or self-converging. Yeah. Like they are making themselves more evident and others need to be actively obscured.

纳瓦尔：这其实回到你前面说的：有些东西能传播，有些不能。会传播的真理，就变成了陈词滥调、常识。会传播的谎言，你知道，那就是假新闻，满世界都是，是政治，是大量争斗的由来。传不开的谎言很快就没了。而还有一类是不传播的真理——这些其实最有意思。它们传不开，是因为传播是群体的功能：要传播，就得有一个供它传播的群体。所以任何削弱群体凝聚力的真理都传不开。于是就有了那么一个二乘二的矩阵，对吧？

Naval: Well, it actually goes back to what you said earlier which is some things are spreadable and some things are not. So when you get a truth that spreads, it's cliché. It's conventional wisdom. If you get a lie that spreads, you know, that's fake news. That's all over. That's politics. That's a lot of the combat that goes on. A lie that doesn't spread just disappears very quickly. And then there's the truths that don't spread. And those are actually the most interesting. And the reason they don't spread is because spread is a function of groups. To spread, there needs to be a group to spread within. So any truth that lowers group cohesion will not spread. And then there's kind of like a 2x2 matrix, right?

埃里克：对，一个「真／假 × 能传播／不能传播」的二乘二矩阵。

Eric: Yeah. It's a 2x2 matrix of truth versus spreadability versus spread.

纳瓦尔：基本上就是常识、假新闻、异端，还有——压根不存在。四种，或者说，胡说八道。

Naval: And basically you have conventional wisdom, fake news, heresies and non-existence. Those are the four—or nonsense I would say.

埃里克：对。

Eric: Yeah.

纳瓦尔：问题在于，人人都知道常识；而异端传不开。所以，新闻里大多数是假新闻。按定义就是这样：常识不用传，因为它早就在那儿了；异端传不动，因为它本来就不具传播性；胡说哪儿也去不了。结果，唯一能在环境里活下来、穿过来的，就是假新闻。

Naval: So, I mean the problem is like everyone knows the conventional wisdom. The heresies don't spread. So, most of the news is fake news. By definition, like conventional wisdom doesn't need to be spread because it's already out there. And heresies don't spread because they're not spreadable and nonsense doesn't go anywhere. So, the only thing that makes it through the environment is fake news.

埃里克：其实——彼得·蒂尔那个问题，就是在挖异端，对吧？

Eric: Well, it's actually—and Peter Thiel's question is digging for heresies, right?

纳瓦尔：没错。你要想「非共识而正确」，就得押注在某个异端上。

Naval: That's right. If you want to be non-consensus correct, you need to bet on a heresy.

埃里克：对。不过彼得只谈商业场景。

Eric: Right. Now, Peter only talks about business contexts.

纳瓦尔：是。但真正的异端到处都是。商业里有，科学里有，还有——遗传学这个领域简直满是异端。

Naval: Yeah. But the real heresies are everywhere. Like they're out there in business. They're out there in science. They're out there in—like the field of genetics is full of heresies.

埃里克：对。人口学也是。

Eric: Yeah. Demographics.

纳瓦尔：对。人口学满是异端，社会科学满是异端，经济学也满是异端。

Naval: Yeah. Demographics full of heresies. Social sciences are full of heresies. Economics is full of heresies.

埃里克：是，太有意思了。我读一段引文，看它有没有被更新，尤其是经由多伊奇：「我的哲学归结为这样：在一端，演化有一条统摄原则，因为它解释了太多关于人的事；另一端是佛教，关于我们的内在状态，它是最古老、最经得起时间检验的精神哲学。」

Eric: Yeah, that's very interesting. I'll read a clip and see if this is updated at all, in particular via Deutsch. "My philosophy falls down to this. On one pole, evolution has a binding principle because it explains so much about humans. On the other is Buddhism, which is the oldest, most time-tested spiritual philosophy regarding our internal state."

纳瓦尔：是，我仍然认这句话。我觉得那两条到现在也都成立。我只是要补上一点：经由多伊奇，量子物理学是我们对所处物质世界最好的解释。还有他的认识论——用「难以改动的好解释」取代其他说法；我再往里加一点点：这些解释往往还能做出冒险而狭窄的预测。这差不多就是你判断真假的尺子，也就是认识论。这是一个极其有用的补充。然后是计算，再然后是这几者之间的联系：量子计算与量子物理、认识论、自然选择演化之间的联系，还有佛教的精神性——这块我知道多伊奇毫无兴趣，也完全不纳入他的世界观。

Naval: Yeah, I mean, I still stand by that. I think those are both still true. I would just add to it that I think yeah through Deutsch, quantum physics is our best explanation for the world that we live in, the material world. His epistemology and his replacement of good explanations that are hard to vary—and I add on to that a little bit, like it helps me to think of it also they often make risky and narrow predictions. That's kind of how you determine what's true from what's false. Epistemology. So that is an incredibly useful addition. And then computation and then the linkages between all of these. So quantum—you know, linkage between quantum computation and quantum physics and epistemology and evolution by natural selection, and then also with Buddhist spirituality, which I know Deutsch has zero interest in and does not factor into his worldview at all.

埃里克：但你哲学里那种务实的部分就在这里——管住我们心智中「作为人」的那部分。佛教在这方面表达得最好。

Eric: But that's where your sort of the practicality of your philosophy comes in, of just like managing the humanness of our minds. Like Buddhism is the best expression of that.

纳瓦尔：对，它很实用，但我同时也认为它是真的。我未必百分之百照它过日子，但在智识上我相当相信佛教的解释是对的：意识就是一切，一切都从意识中生起；自我是一种虚构。我日常真的照这样活吗？……但我确实暂且相信它为真，而且我觉得它比「意识从物质中生起」是更好的模型。我倒觉得物质是从意识中生起的。

Naval: Yeah, it's practical, but I also think it's true. I don't necessarily live my life 100% this way, but intellectually I'm pretty convinced that the Buddhist interpretation is correct, that consciousness is everything and everything arises within consciousness, that the self is a fiction. Do I live my actual life that way day-to-day? But I do believe it to be true tentatively, and I think it is a better model than saying that consciousness arises within matter. I think matter arises within consciousness.

埃里克：这和多伊奇相容吗？我是说他那套「人类例外论」——我们是已知唯一有意识的存在。

Eric: Is that compatible with Deutsch? I mean his thing about the human exceptionalism and we are the only known conscious beings.

纳瓦尔：完全相容。唯一判定不了的是：到底是一切都发生在我的意识里，还是我的意识发生在一切里？据我们所知，没有这样的实验，对吧。

Naval: It's completely compatible. It's the one thing you can't determine is like is everything taking place in my consciousness or is my consciousness taking place in everything? Like there is no experiment we know of, you know.

埃里克：对。这事有可能知道吗？

Eric: Yeah. Will that ever be knowable?

纳瓦尔：我不想说「永远不可能」，因为也许有人会极有创造力；但我一时想不出区分二者的办法。多伊奇有一个针对量子多元宇宙的实验——量子计算其实就是这么被他想出来的。人们没意识到这点：他提出量子计算理论，是因为他想为量子多元宇宙设计一个可证伪的实验。为此，他得造一个 AGI，把它当成观察者，钻进它的「脑子」里，看看波函数坍缩到底是发生在观察者内部，还是由观察者引发。而要做到这个，他说：那我需要一个量子 AGI。要量子 AGI，就需要量子计算机。于是他把量子计算的方程推了出来。量子计算就是这么出生的。

Naval: I don't want to say never, because someone might get very creative, but I can't think of a way offhand to distinguish between the two. Deutsch has an experiment for the quantum multiverse, which is actually how he came up with quantum computing. People don't realize this, but he came up with the theory of quantum computation because he was trying to create a falsifiability experiment for the quantum multiverse. And so to do that, he had to create an AGI and treat that as an observer and get inside its brain and then see if wave-function collapse happened inside the observer rather than from the observer. And to do that, he was like, well then I need a quantum AGI. And for a quantum AGI, I need a quantum computer. So then he worked out the equations for quantum computing. That's how quantum computing was born.

埃里克：太疯了。

Eric: That's crazy.

纳瓦尔：是。从量子物理、从多元宇宙理论里长出来的。所以我不想把话说死，因为他真的搞成了——他是在试图为量子多元宇宙找一个可证伪实验的过程中，硬生生发明了量子计算这个领域。所以，也许真会有一个可证伪的实验，能区分「物质存在于意识之中」和「意识存在于物质之中」。

Naval: Yeah. From quantum physics, from the multiverse theory. So I don't want to say never because he figured out—he literally invented the field of quantum computing while trying to figure out a falsifiability experiment for the quantum multiverse. So there may be a falsifiable experiment to distinguish whether materiality exists inside consciousness or consciousness exists inside materiality.

埃里克：这简直是一条史诗级的支线任务。

Eric: That's a pretty all-time side quest.

纳瓦尔：正是。不过连他的实验目前也还跑不起来。你得先有 AGI，还得先有量子计算机，对吧。

Naval: Exactly. But even his experiment is not runnable yet. You know it's you would need an AGI first and you need quantum computers first.

埃里克：你以前说过，想哪天写篇博客，讲怎么把佛教的教义直接映射到虚拟现实模拟上。

Eric: You mentioned before that you wanted to write a blog post at some point about how you can map the tenets of Buddhism directly onto a virtual-reality simulation.

纳瓦尔：哦，该死，我把这条给忘了。对。大致思路应该是这样：假设你活在模拟里，对吧？比方说你明天发现——你在《黑客帝国》里。心里扎了根刺：「我在矩阵里，我得出去。」这基本上就对应着对开悟的探求：你在找真相，却不知道真相是什么。这就是《黑客帝国》整部片子讲的事，对吧？再假设你真逃出来了，对吧？结果你落进了另一层矩阵——锡安。你怎么知道锡安不是另一个矩阵？这就成了《盗梦空间》问题：你怎么知道自己在哪层梦里？因为你能出来，就说明「出来」是可能的，那你也就没法相信眼前这一层是真的，对吧？它也可能只是个模拟。所以逃出模拟对你没帮助，只会把你丢进一个无限套娃。于是你永远在试图逃出矩阵；无论你在哪儿，永远不知道自己是不是在「底层现实」。只有一种情况能让你确信自己在底层现实：此刻你也许是死的——但要是死了，就没人可被说服了，你也不可能拥有「死了」的体验，所以这不算。真正逃出矩阵只有一条路，就是白屋。在《黑客帝国》里，他们走进白屋，屋子里一片空白。那是什么？那就是意识。除了纯粹的觉知，那里什么都没有，对吧？如果你处在纯粹觉知里，就等于你没有在模拟任何东西——你只是在那儿。就这样。你唯一还在模拟的，是「我」。佛教里与之对应的是：当你意识到自己只是纯粹意识，其他一切都只是来来去去的形相。其他一切都是矩阵，对吧？全是模拟。如果你用的是这个模型，那你就已经开悟了，就相当于待在矩阵里的白屋。二者在功能上是一回事：你剩下的只是纯粹意识。而那真的很无聊。

Naval: Oh, shoot. I'd forgotten that one. Yeah. I think the way it kind of goes is roughly something like if you were living in a sim, right? Let's say you found out tomorrow. You're living in the Matrix. There's a splinter in your mind like I'm in the Matrix. I need to get out, right? So that would basically be like the quest for enlightenment. You'd be looking for truth. You didn't know what it was. This is what the Matrix is all about, right? And then you would find out, let's say you break out of it, right? And now you land in another matrix. You land in Zion. How do you know that's not another matrix, right? You have an Inception problem. How do you know which dream you're in? Which dream level you're in? Because the moment you're out, it means you can get out, which means that you can't trust this is real either, right? This could just be a sim as well. So breaking out of the simulation does you no favors. It puts you in an infinite trap. And so then you're always trying to break out of the Matrix. No matter where you are, you never know if you're base reality or not. So there's only one way you could be convinced you're base reality. Right now, you could be dead, but if you're dead, there's no one to convince. You can't have an experience of being dead. So that's not valid. But there's only one way to truly break out of the Matrix, which is the white room. In the Matrix, they go into the white room. It's just blank. What is that? It's just consciousness. There's nothing there other than just that pure awareness, right? If you're in pure awareness, that means you're not simulating anything other than just you're there. That's it. The only thing you're simulating is I. So the equivalent to that in Buddhism is when you realize you're just pure consciousness and everything else is just forms that come and go. Everything else is Matrix, right? It's all simulation. Now, if you're that model, you're enlightened and you're in the white room in the Matrix. They're functionally equivalent. All you are is pure consciousness. It's really boring.

那开悟之前和开悟之后，你做什么？有一句禅话说：开悟前，砍柴挑水；开悟后，砍柴挑水。所以现在你意识到：无聊了。你也明白了为什么矩阵——或者说白屋——会生成整个矩阵，为什么上帝会造出无穷的形式，为什么「你」作为上帝会忘了自己，又钻回游戏里。因为外面太无聊了。于是你成了塞弗。佛教和模拟理论，多少把你带到同一个地方。假设我们真造出了模拟，把自己放了进去：一开始会特别好玩。你会说：我在模拟里，我什么都能干。然后你又说：等等，我控制着这个模拟。太无聊了，对吧？于是你不得不忘了自己在模拟里；你得在模拟里把自己的记忆抹掉，这样才能真正享受它。可接着你又会害怕，到某个时刻生出一种紧张：我得出去。等你真想出去，又会问：我怎么知道我出去了？最后你到了白屋，说：现在我出去了。可这里又无聊得要命，于是你再造一个模拟，抹掉记忆，一头扎回去。这就是你的轮回，你那无穷无尽的轮回。我就是这样把佛教映射到模拟理论上的。结论一样，但模拟理论在我看来是个糟糕的理论：它不可证伪，只是把皮球往前踢。它给出的只是一种概率论证，大意是：嘿，我们很可能会先发展出这个，再干那个，然后再这样、再那样。可它有太多可能大幅出错的地方。比如模拟的分辨率总会低于现实，外面的人总能控制里面的人。问题一大堆。再加上我认为人天生就渴求真相。所以我不觉得谁愿意活在谎言里……就算是我自己，假设活在某种别的现实里，我也不会造一个让自己忘掉一切的假现实。就算造了，我也会给自己留条后路、留个退出键，只是我得非常努力才能找到它。也许那就是开悟，对吧？

And so what do you do before enlightenment or after enlightenment? Well, there's a Zen saying like before enlightenment, chop wood, carry water. After enlightenment, chop wood, carry water. So now you realize you're bored. And now you realize why the Matrix or the white room generates the entire Matrix, or why God creates infinite forms, or why you know you as God forget yourself and then go back into the game. It's 'cause it's boring out there. So you go be Cipher. So Buddhism and sim theory kind of lead you the same way. So, if we managed to create simulations and we put ourselves inside the simulation, at first it would be really fun. You'd be like, I'm in a sim. I can do anything. And you're like, wait, I control the sim. This is really boring, right? And so then you'd have to forget that you're in a sim. You'd have to wipe your own memory in the sim so that you can actually enjoy the sim, but then you'd also feel fear and at some point you get this nervous feel like I need to get out of the sim. And then when you try to get out of the sim, you're like, how do I know I'm out of the sim? And then eventually you get to the white room, you're like, now I'm out of the sim. But now this is really boring and you create a sim, wipe your memory and dive back in. That's your rebirths. That's your infinite cycle of rebirths. So that's how I map Buddhism to simulation theory. The same outcome, but simulation theory to me is a lousy theory. It's unfalsifiable. You know, it just kicks the can up the road. All it makes is a probabilistic argument. It says like, hey, we're likely to develop this and then do that and then do this and then do that. But there are too many ways it could go wrong in a big way. Like the sim is always going to be lower resolution than reality. The people on the outside can always control the people on the inside. So there are all kinds of problems with it. Plus I think we inherently have a craving for truth. So I don't think anybody wants to live a lie. Even I myself, like even if I were you know living in some other reality I would not create a fake reality where I would forget everything. Or if I did I'd leave myself an out and an eject button which I'd have to look really hard to find. Maybe that's called enlightenment, right?

埃里克：我倒觉得模拟理论是个有用的信念，因为你看看那些人——如果你从人生里需要的是别太把自己当回事、要有高能动性、要行动但别为行动的目标过度焦虑，那它对大半个人生其实是个挺好用的框架。它就是宗教。

Eric: Well, I like that the simulation theory I find is a useful belief because you see the people—if what you need out of life is to not take yourself too seriously, to be high-agency, to be active but not stress too much about what you're being active about. Like it's actually a pretty useful frame for most of life. It's just religion.

纳瓦尔：宗教能给你一模一样的东西。真的。步骤还更少，涉及的计算更少，程序员也更少。区别只是你不叫它上帝，而叫它伟大的程序员。

Naval: Religion would do the exact same thing for you. Exactly. With less steps, less computation involved, less programmers. So instead of calling it God, you're just calling it the great programmer.

埃里克：对。而且朋友大概还更多。

Eric: Yeah. And more friends probably.

纳瓦尔：对。社会里还有这么一种倾向：不管最新的科学世界观是什么，我们都把它叠到宗教上面，对吧？从前是太阳，人的生死都靠太阳，于是就有了太阳神拉；后来国王掌权，就有了神王；牛顿出来以后，我们有了机械宇宙，于是人们说：哦，宇宙像一只大钟，是机械论的宇宙，上帝死了。现在计算机当道，于是宇宙又成了一台大计算机。

Naval: Yeah. So there's also a tendency in society that whatever the latest scientific worldview is, we layer that onto religion, right? So when it was, you know, the sun, you lived and died by the sun, then the sun god Ra was a sun god, right? And then when the king was in charge then it was a god-king. And when Newton came along we got the mechanical universe, you know, then it was like oh the universe like a big watch. It's a mechanistic universe. God is dead. And now because computers are ascendant, you know, the universe is a big computer.

埃里克：对。我们有办法把永恒的问题，打扮成当代的问题。

Eric: Yeah. We have a way of taking the permanent problems and making them contemporary.

纳瓦尔：对。我们总是透过那个时代最先进知识的透镜去看它。不过我会说，理解相对论和量子物理这类东西、研究宇宙学、研究物理，比一句「宇宙是台计算机」更让人满足。说宇宙是计算机，几乎还原得过头了，太用力了。举个很简单的例子：计算机是状态机，状态机有步骤，步骤意味着一切都是离散的——这件事发生，那件事发生，如果这样，就走那条分支。可一切都排在一个离散的序列里，这大致是量子物理的说法：量子计算、量子物理说宇宙是离散的。但相对论不是这么说的，相对论说宇宙是连续的。所以矛盾就在这儿：任何声称宇宙是计算机的模拟理论，都依赖于一种并不存在的计算——连量子计算也算不上，它违反了相对论。这就是为什么所有基于计算的世界理论，比如斯蒂芬·沃尔夫勒姆那套计算物理学，都会撞上同一个问题：实在是连续的。然后他又搬出另一整套东西，那个超图，我不太懂，但我总觉得它是太顺手地把我们时代的主导范式套到宗教上，再把上帝挪开、藏到幕布后面。

Naval: Yeah. We always view it through the contemporary lens of our greatest knowledge at that time. Yeah. But I would argue that like understanding things like relativity and quantum physics and you know studying cosmology and just sort of physics is more satisfying than saying the universe is a computer. Saying the universe is a computer is almost too reductive. You know it's trying too hard. Like as a very simple example, a computer is a state machine. A state machine has steps. Steps means everything is discrete. This happens and this happens and that happens. If this happens then take that branch, take that branch. But everything is in a discrete sequence and that is kind of what quantum physics says. So quantum computation, quantum physics says the universe is discrete. But that is not what relativity says. Relativity says the universe is continuous. And so right there you run into a contradiction: like any sim theory that says the universe is a computer relies upon a kind of computation that does not exist. Not even quantum computation. It violates relativity. And this is why all the compute-based theories of the world like Stephen Wolfram's, you know, compute-based physics, they run into this issue which is that reality is continuous. So then he's got this whole other thing, this hypergraph thing which I don't fully understand, but I just feel like it's too conveniently mapping the dominant paradigm of our times onto religion and then removing and then hiding God behind the curtain.

埃里克：对。这一点我们该警惕。

Eric: Yeah. Which is we should be suspicious of.

纳瓦尔：对。我觉得更简单的做法是——这种事你必须自己有看法，因为别人谁也满足不了你，别的东西也满足不了你。而且我觉得，一旦你能给它安上一个名字，或者给它安上一个结构，它就有点问题了。

Naval: Yeah. I think it's easier just to—this is the kind of thing you have to have your own viewpoint on because no one else will satisfy you. Nothing else will satisfy you. And I think if you can put a name to it, it's kind of problematic, or if you can put a structure to it, it's problematic.

埃里克：我真的很喜欢这条推文，拿它为你讲的许多东西收尾，因为我觉得它把好几条看似矛盾的线索拴在了一起：为比你自己更大的东西而活，但只按你自己的方式。

Eric: I really love this tweet as a capstone for a lot of what you've shared because I feel like it ties together a lot of threads that might seem to have an apparent contradiction, which is: live for something larger than yourself, but only on your own terms.

纳瓦尔：哦，这条我喜欢。我把它忘了。我喜欢这条。为比你自己更大的东西而活，但只——你知道吗？我就要照这个去做。我喜欢这条。它真是把一切理清楚的好办法。是啊，因为社会不愿告诉你「按自己的方式活」，它怕你把社会搞砸，怕你变成罪犯、暴徒、享乐主义者。但那是脑子不清醒的人走的路。你知道，不知道自己在干什么的人，才会吸毒过量、犯太多法、做可怕的事。你不需要那样。那不会让你快乐，那只是个陷阱，一个把你困住的陷阱。你看一个瘾君子，就是困在这样的陷阱里；你看到有人犯罪，也是一样——想得到你想要的东西，有更好的办法。你不那么做，日子反而会过得更好。很多进步主义就是从这儿来的。你当然还得有惩罚来劝阻人，但与此同时，我觉得你确实得按自己的方式活，否则你根本不会去做；你也确实得为比自己更大的东西而活，否则结局会很糟，你也不会快乐。所以，对，我就照这条建议做。谢谢你。

Naval: Oh, I like that one. I'd forgotten about that one. I like that one. Live for something larger than yourself, but only—you know what? I'm going to go do exactly that. I like that one. It's a really good way to disentangle everything. Yeah, 'cause society doesn't want to tell you to live on your own terms because they're afraid you're going to screw over society. You're just going to be a criminal and a thug and a hedonist. But that's a path for unintelligent people. You know, the person who doesn't know what they're doing is the one doing too many drugs or breaking too many laws or doing horrible things. Like, you don't need to do that. You know, it's not going to make you happy. It's just a trap. It's a trap that you're caught in. You know, when we look at a drug addict, that's a trap that they're caught in. The same way you see someone committing crimes, like there are better ways to get what you want. You will actually have a better life if you don't do that. And that's where like a lot of progressivism comes from. You know, you still got to have punishments to dissuade people, but at the same time, I feel like you do have to live life on your own terms or you're not going to do it, but you have to live for something larger than yourself or it's going to lead to a very bad outcome and you're not going to be happy. So, yeah, I'm going to follow that advice. Thank you for that.

埃里克：我是说，你要是自己都不上头，那它就不是——那可不是什么好料。

纳瓦尔：我发现自己写的推文，然后说：哇，这条真不错。你知道，一本书差不多可以交付的信号就是这样：你重新拿起来读，发现它比你记忆里还好。

Naval: I love finding my own tweet and being like, wow, that's really good. You know, that's how I know a book is almost ready when I pick it up again and it's better than I remembered it.

埃里克：对。伟大的书也是分形的，你每次都能学到点新东西。

Eric: Yeah. The great books are fractal, too. They like you learn something new every time.

纳瓦尔：比如我现在喜欢做的一件事：我找到一些——你知道，像叔本华和塞内卡，他们的东西有不错的有声书和录音，可他们是密度极高的作者和讲述者。你听他们的东西会漏掉很多，因为这些本来是写出来的，是给人读的。这就是为什么我发过一条推文说：听书而不读书，有点像把蔬菜榨成汁喝下去，而不是嚼着吃，对吧？效率是高了，但你得到的大概只是一阵糖分上头——不是按它本来的方式摄入，好处拿不全，不过总比什么都没有强，对吧？总比不去读它强。所以我最近一直在听一批本来只该读的作者。我得跟自己和解的一件事是：我大概只能接收到 20% 的通量。尤其叔本华，每一句都是精心打磨的，每一个念头都是精心打磨的，那家伙是天才。你按说话的速度听他，我就：等等，停一下；哦，我刚漏掉了那句；哦，那是什么？他指的是什么？哦，我走神了。于是不停地按暂停。但最后我只好放弃较劲：算了，我能听进去什么就听什么，能吸收多少就吸收多少，以后再回头听一遍。因为那东西真的好到值得。而我意识到，其实读书时也是一回事：你读一段，能捞到一句就算走运，而且每次捞到的可能还是不同的一句。但好书有个特点——没什么废话，每一句在某个时刻、对某个人都有用。所以我在作者身上非常看重这一点，叔本华这方面多得很。同一篇文章，我可以在五年里读十遍，每次都能从中拿到不同的东西。

Naval: Like one of the things I like doing now is I found some, you know, Schopenhauer and Seneca, for example, they have good—they're good audiobooks and recordings of their stuff, but they're very high-density speakers and authors. So if you listen to their stuff, you miss a lot because it was written so it's meant to be read. Which is why I had this tweet about like, you know, listening to books instead of reading them is like drinking your vegetables instead of eating them, right? Like it's more efficient, but you're getting kind of a sugar high. You're not taking it the way it was meant to be. You're not getting all the benefits, but it's better than nothing, right? It's better to do that than to like not consume it. And so I've been listening to a bunch of authors who are normally written down. And one of the things I had to come to peace with was just like, you know what, I'm only going to get like 20% of the throughput. Like Schopenhauer especially, every sentence is crafted. Every thought is crafted. That guy is a genius. And so when you listen to him at spoken speed, I'm wait, stop. Oh, I missed that. Oh, what was that? What was he referring to? Oh, I got lost in a daydream. So I'm constantly pausing it. But then I finally had to give up on I was just like, you know, I'm just going to listen to what I'm going to listen to. I'm going to absorb what I'm going to absorb and I'll just go back and listen to it again later. Like it was that good. And what I've realized is even when reading you have the same issue. You read a paragraph, you'll be lucky if you pick up a sentence and it might be a different sentence every time, but the good books have the property that there's not much wasted space. All the sentences are useful at some point to somebody. And so I really value that in an author and Schopenhauer has it in spades. I can go back and read the same essay 10 times over five years and I'll get something different out of it every time.

埃里克：对。你有那句，你知道，把最好的一百本书一遍又一遍地读。

Naval: Yeah. There's no point in reading the rest. Yeah. But the thing I can't do is like read contemporary history or sociology or, you know, there are these very smart people who seem to get a lot of value out of reading a lot of history and I've read some history but I don't need to read the details of the Roman Empire. You know I love Will Durant. I love The Lessons of History. I'm not going to read the whole Story of Civilization, all 12 volumes. It's like too much detail.

埃里克：他别的书，像《落叶》《历史英雄》那些，真正压缩过的。

纳瓦尔：对，《落叶》有点伤感，我没读下去。开了个头，感觉他像在说：哦，我老了，这真糟，这一切到底为了什么？这本书大体就在说这个。它把他放在了一种我不想看到的光里。也许后面会好些，但我读不完。顺便说一句，就算只读《历史的教训》也不行——你得先读过足够多的具体历史，才能在读到的时候认出来：哦这个模式我见过。我年轻时读过不少历史，所以读《历史的教训》时我知道他在指什么，心里大致有数；但我不会——你知道，我现在没工夫去读十八卷成吉思汗，太晚了。我会读一卷埃隆，因为它跟我当下的世界高度相关。至于成吉思汗的教训，到这个阶段就得更高度地提纯。我们活在 TikTok 一代。

Naval: Yeah, Fallen Leaves is kind of sad. I couldn't make it through it. I started it and it was just like he was like, oh I'm getting old and this sucks and what was the point of all this? Like that's what the book is basically saying. They cast him in a light that I didn't want to see him in. Maybe it gets better later, but I couldn't finish it. And by the way, even The Lessons of History, if all you read was The Lessons of History, that would not work. I've seen, you know, wealth is best pursued by exploring your genuine curiosity. Learning is best done by, you know, reading whatever you're interested in. Even status is best pursued by serving others. Happiness is elusive if it's pursued too directly.

纳瓦尔：对。也许并非一切都得间接追求，但那些看起来特别难抓的东西，之所以难抓，正因为它们得间接追求，对吧？我们已经想明白的那些，反而不拿出来谈，因为可以直接追。看起来难抓的，是因为只能绕着走。不同的东西，原因也不同。财富之所以如此，是因为它是一场竞争性游戏：真正投入自己所做之事的人，永远会胜过不投入的人。间接性有一部分就是从这儿来的。再有，如果你做的事纯粹是为了自己，你就招不到最好的人。比如你看色情、赌博、加密货币，尤其是加密里赌场那一块，你拿不到最好的团队。好团队也不会来，因为最优秀的人不想干那些，也不想跟你一起干。于是你所在的行业里，陪着你的——抱歉我直说——是一批素质不高的人。你的生活质量会因此下降，身边的人道德感更弱，也更享乐。幸福那一头则是：你不能直接追它，因为直接追求快感会导致成瘾，成瘾带来不幸福，多巴胺被烧空。所以对幸福的直接追求根本行不通，它把你想要好好调节的那个受体烧坏了，你知道，就是那个你想让它压过其他冲动的受体。

Naval: Yeah, it may not be that everything is best pursued indirectly, but the things that seem very elusive are that way because they're best pursued indirectly, right? The stuff we have figured out, we don't talk about because we can pursue it directly. But the stuff that seems elusive is because it's best pursued indirectly. And there are different reasons for different things. In the case of wealth, it's because it's a competitive game. So someone who's really into what they're doing will always outcompete someone who's not into it. So that's where part of the indirectness comes from. And then if you're doing something purely selfish, you won't get the best people on your side. Like for example, if you look at like porn or gambling or crypto, like especially the casino-ish part of crypto, you don't get the best teams. You don't get good teams 'cause the best people don't want to do that stuff. They don't want to work with you. So now you're in an industry where you just have like, sorry if I call them out, but low-quality people working alongside you. And then you're just going to have a lower quality of life and you're going to have people who are less ethical and kind of like you know more hedonistic. In the case of happiness, you can't pursue it directly because the direct pursuit of pleasure causes addiction and that leads to unhappiness, that causes dopamine burnout. So the direct pursuit of that simply doesn't work. It burns out the receptor, you know, that you're trying to like modulate in a good way over the others.

埃里克：地位最好靠服务和支持他人来获得。

Eric: Status is best pursued by serving and supporting others.

纳瓦尔：对。地位没法直接追求，因为地位是等级，而且是一种隐藏的等级。一个人到底有多少地位，很难看得出来，得别人自己品出来。而嘴上说自己地位高，本身就是低地位——真有地位，你不必说，人人都知道。所以直接追求地位，反而是一个降地位的动作。这就是为什么人们贬低别人时，总爱摆出反讽或机智的姿态。他们想靠说「哦，埃隆是纳粹」「他很坏」「他做了那个敬礼」把人踩下去。我就想：不，你只是个中智者，你是个傻瓜。你该去看看那段视频，看看人们挥舞手臂时，一天里做出多少所谓的敬礼。你证明的只是自己是个傻瓜。他们真正想做的是拉低他的地位，好抬高自己的地位——用间接的方式去追。所以我们在记者身上、在推特上、在现代这套假新闻里看到的大量恶劣行为，不过是隐蔽的地位追逐。至于爱——你不会想直接去追求「被爱」，因为那份爱是索取来的，而且它其实不是你想要的东西。你真正想要的是给予爱。爱这东西，你随时都能自己造出来。我其实羡慕——加引号的羡慕，不算真羡慕——有一类人就是特别有爱，能给出真诚的爱，却从不挂在嘴上。那些开口闭口「爱爱爱」、把这个词挂在嘴边的人，是在追地位，对吧？那只是追地位的另一种方式。而那些真的能感受爱、能把爱给到很多人的人，那是一种开悟者的样子。他们自己看上去总是挺幸福的。你上一次见到一个极有爱、却又活得很痛苦的人，是什么时候？

Naval: Yeah. Status can't be pursued directly because status is hierarchy and it's a hidden hierarchy. Like it's very hard to tell what status someone is. They have to glean it out. And saying that you're high status is low status because if you're high status, you don't have to say it. Everyone knows it. And so directly pursuing status is a status-lowering activity. So that's why people try to be ironic or clever when they put people down. You know, they try to bring people down by saying, oh, Elon's a Nazi. He's evil. You know, he did the salute. I'm like, no, you're a midwit. You're an idiot. You should watch the video and see how many so-called salutes people do all day long when they're waving their arms around. So all you've proven is you're an idiot. But what they're trying to do is they're trying to bring him down in status. And so they're trying to elevate their own status. They're trying to pursue it indirectly. So a lot of what we see as bad behavior from journalists or on Twitter or modern-side with fake news is just the pursuit of status in a covert way. And love—you don't want to pursue directly receiving love because love is needy and it's actually not the thing you want. It's giving love that is the thing that you want. You can manufacture love anytime you want. I actually envy—envy, you know, quotes, not really envy—but there's a class of person who's just very loving, you know, and can give genuine love without talking about it. The people who are, you know, saying oh love love love, they use the word a lot, they're pursuing status, right? It's just another way to pursue status. But the people who are genuinely capable of feeling and giving love to large quantities of people, like that's a form of an enlightened person. And they themselves always seem pretty happy. When's the last time you saw a person who's extremely loving but miserable?

埃里克：这和多伊奇的「乐趣标准」有关系吗？这名字起得不好，但它是——

Eric: Is this related to Deutsch's fun criterion? It's not a good brand, but it's—

纳瓦尔：哦，有。不，多伊奇有一个独立版本，讲的是同一个道理。他叫它「乐趣标准」：如果不好玩，就别做。他的版本更极端一点，比如他说别教孩子数学——这是「认真对待孩子」那套东西的一部分：孩子不喜欢，就别教他数学。我就想，见鬼，我的孩子会变成数学盲，等他们大到能自己捡起来的时候，已经太晚了。所以我不确定自己能把它贯彻到底，但我明白他的意思，也相信它极有价值。对，他叫它乐趣标准：不好玩就别做。我觉得他是对的。

Naval: Oh, yeah. No, Deutsch has an independent version of the same principle. He calls it the fun criterion, which is like if you're not having fun, don't do it. Now, his is a little more extreme. Like his like no, don't teach, you know, this is part of the Taking Children Seriously thing. Like don't teach your kids math if they don't enjoy it. I'm like, goddamn it, my kids are going to be mathematically innumerate and then too old to, you know, to pick it up later. So, I'm not sure I follow it the whole way, but I get what he's saying and I believe there's huge merit to it. Yeah. He calls it the fun criterion. If you're not having fun, don't do it. And I think he's right.

就像，我年纪越大，做的事越好玩，反而越真诚。我学得一点没少；要说的话，学得更多了。只是我不再因为「不知道本该知道的东西」而苛责自己。结果变成：我最后学到的，都是我真感兴趣的东西，然后越学越多，越学越快。这其实跟我又开始做创业公司有关——我现在在做一家非常难的公司。公司叫「不可能计算机公司」。好吧，真的很难。我们想做的事真的很难，说不定根本不可能。字面意义上，公司就叫 Impossible Inc.，我们有 ImpossibleComputer.com。所以我们做的事基本上就是不可能。上周我跟四个人聊过，都是各自领域的技术专家，稍微讲了我们在做什么。四个人全都告诉我这不可能——四个完全不同的理由，对吧？所以这不是件容易的事。但我告诉你，我这辈子从没学得这么快，因为我对它太好奇了。每次冒出一个问题，我就：好，把论文调出来；好，ChatGPT，开 o3 Pro 引擎，还有 Gemini 2.5 Pro，来吧，教我这个，教我这个；不对不对，像给五岁小孩那样解释；不对不对，退回去，这个呢？我给联合创始人打电话：我们来开个白板会。我觉得自己像个傻瓜：这是什么？你什么意思？你没跟我说过这个。哦，你想当然了。不，这才是难的部分，你知道？所以我学得非常多，原因就是我有动力。我有动力出去学。我确实有点动力：我希望那个答案是个好答案，但我并不是被答案本身真正驱动。我想在想法的迷宫里给自己导航——在那片深渊里走出来，找到对的答案。真正驱动学习曲线的，是「试着去做、试着造出来」这个具体动作。没有那个非常具体的活动，我的学习速度会远远不如现在。那种动力真的很重要，它实实在在地把我的学习曲线变陡了。其实我部分戒了咖啡因、多少回到更健康的状态，也是因为我想一直清醒、一直在场，这样才跟得上这家公司。我得跟上它。

Like, the older I get, the more I do things that are more fun, the more genuine it is. I'm not learning any less. If anything, I'm learning more. It's just I don't beat myself up anymore about not knowing about things that I should know about. And instead what ends up happening is I end up learning the things that I'm genuinely interested in and then I learn more. I learn faster. Actually this is related to, you know, I've started doing startups again and I'm doing a very difficult company right now. The name of the company is the Impossible Computer Company. Okay. It's really hard. What we're trying to do is really hard. It might be impossible. And literally the name of the company is Impossible Inc. But you know we have ImpossibleComputer.com. So what we're doing is basically impossible. I talked to four people last week, technical experts, each in their domain, a little bit about what we're doing. All four of them told me it was impossible—all four told me it was impossible for four completely different reasons, right? So, it's not an easy task. But I'll tell you, I've never learned faster in my life because I'm so curious about it. Like, every time something comes up, I'm like, all right, let me pull up the paper. All right, ChatGPT, fire up the o3 Pro engines, you know, fire up Gemini 2.5 Pro. Let's go. Teach me this. Teach me this. No, no, explain like I'm five. No, no, back up. What about this? I call my co-founder. I'm like, we're doing a whiteboard session. I feel like an idiot. What is this? What do you mean? You didn't tell me this. Oh, you take it for granted. No, this is the hardest part, you know? So, I'm learning so much, but because I'm motivated. I'm motivated to go out and learn. I am a little motivated. I want the answer to be a good answer, but I'm not truly motivated by the answer. I want to navigate my way through the idea maze, you know, the abyss of the idea maze, and figure out the right answer. But the concrete act of trying to do something and trying to build something is driving the learning curve. If I didn't have that very concrete activity, then I would not be learning at anywhere near the same rate. And that motivation has been really important. It's really accelerated my learning curve. Actually, I'm not drinking caffeine partially and I'm kind of back on more of a health kick is because I want to be alert and awake all the time so I can just stay up with the company. I can keep up with it.

埃里克：而且我敢肯定，还有一层额外的动力：你在造某种从来没人造出来过的东西。

Eric: Well, and you're additionally motivated, I'm sure, by building something that has never been built before.

纳瓦尔：正是。而且我在往里砸——现在我们每个月烧掉一百五十万美元，是我的钱。这又多了一层动力。

Naval: Exactly. And I'm pouring—right now we're burning a million and a half dollars a month of my money. So, that's an additional layer of motivation.

埃里克：这也是动力。

Eric: That's motivation, too.

纳瓦尔：对。正是。对。

Naval: Yeah. Exactly. Yeah.

埃里克：你现在学得飞快——钱都花在哪儿了？你刚签的那张五十万美元的支票是怎么回事？

Eric: You're learning very fast when you've got—what are we spending money on? What is that half-million-dollar check you just wrote?

纳瓦尔：哦，好吧。该死。

埃里克：所以你既有一根大胡萝卜，也有一根大棒——这是学习的好配方。

纳瓦尔：非常激励人。对，对。这事上我很受埃隆启发。那个故事，你知道，说他卖掉 PayPal 拿到两亿美元，拿一亿投了 SpaceX，八千万投特斯拉，两千万投 SolarCity，最后还得借钱付房租。我不知道是不是真的，但这是个很棒的谎言，特别激励人。所以我想：既然你能这么干，我也能下一注。

Naval: Highly motivating. Yeah. Yeah. Well, I was very inspired by Elon on this. I don't know if that's true but it's a great lie. Very inspirational. So I'm like, well if you can do that I can make a bet too.

埃里克：而且那个故事还把事情说简单了，因为它是分几轮发生的——对任何站在燃烧平台上的人来说，那比一次性押上痛苦得多。他手里坐着两亿美元，先投一个亿，觉得足够把这几家公司办起来、送它们上道了。然后它们快死了，两家看着都到了鬼门关。他不得不把剩下的钱都开出支票，连房子也算上。几乎同时，实际上就是靠一根鞋带、一根细线，让两家都吊着命——而两家都活下来了。

Eric: Well, and that story even oversimplifies it a little because it happened iteratively, which for anybody who's on a burning platform knows like that's way more painful than a one-time. So, he's sitting there with $200 million and he puts a hundred down and says that should be plenty to start these companies and get them there. And then they're going to die. They both look like they're on the verge of death. And he has to write checks for all of his remaining money, including his house. Yeah. Like at the same time, functionally, to keep them both alive by a shoestring, by a thread, and they both lived.

纳瓦尔：对。我不知道这些故事有多真，但它们真带劲，伙计。弗雷德·史密斯也有一个，你知道，联邦快递的创始人：他得拿着最后一笔钱去拉斯维加斯赌一把，好发工资，而他真去了。还有史蒂夫·乔布斯，离开苹果之后把大量身家投了进去——他其实没那么多钱，早年的苹果没那么值钱——但他把钱沉进了皮克斯、沉进了 NeXT，因为那些都是自筹的。有意思的是，乔布斯之后，罗斯·佩罗成了 NeXT 的大金主。可你知道，他在那上面差点栽进去。所以这不是说你必须押上钱才能成事，更多的只是：在没人相信的时候，你得对自己的信念有那份勇气。你得把它从零做到一。你知道，零到一之间的空间是无限的。要跨过那一跳，得押上一切。

Naval: Yeah. I don't know how real these stories are but they're good, man. Fred Smith has one, you know, the founder of FedEx, of how like he had to go gamble the last money in Vegas to make payroll and he did. Yeah. To make that leap requires everything.

埃里克：这是资本的好用法。你想想，那些收益除了投进一项你相信的、在做前所未有之事的使命，还能拿来干什么？

纳瓦尔：可以买一幢超大房子，然后俯视所有人。

不管人们能替埃隆安上什么动机——你要是想往坏处想，可以安一个自我膨胀的动机，但你安不上一个自私的动机。他不是那种想把东西囤起来的人。

Eric: Yeah. World's richest man was a byproduct, not a goal for him. But because—similar to what you're doing—it is easily overlooked that he did the pre-seed and the seed and the A and the B for both Tesla and SpaceX basically himself, right? So not only founder but biggest funder.

我这里还有两条压顶石推文。「要么去创造财富、做出被动收入，要么出家，要么做那件你爱它胜过爱钱的事。剩下的，就是驯服身心、探求真理、创造爱与艺术。世界没有什么可给你的，而你是自由的。」

I got two capstone tweets here. "Either create wealth or passive income or become a monk or do what you love more than money. What remains is taming the mind and the body, seeking truth, creating love and art. The world has nothing to offer you and you are free."

纳瓦尔：这清单可真大。

纳瓦尔：好，第一行就是我给退休下的定义。如果你在做自己爱的事，或者出了家，或者有被动收入，或者有了财富，那你现在就可以退休了，基本上就算完成了。退休之后，剩下的就是驯服身心、探求真理、创造爱与艺术。

Like if you're doing something you love or if you're a monk or if you have a passive income or if you have wealth, now you can retire, like you're basically done. 埃里克：对。没错。

Eric: Yeah. Right.

纳瓦尔：那是一条更容易的路。我想我是想找到幸福、灵性上的开悟，还有所有那些东西，但我不愿意为此交出我的世界。我是个贪心的人，对吧？我也许是世上最贪的人，因为我想当——你知道，我想当埃隆，想当成功的创业者；想当蒂尔，想当成功的投资人；想要一个特别好的家庭生活；想尽可能久地保持健康；我也不介意出名，你知道。但我也想要开悟，这个我不愿意放弃。所以对我来说，你知道，那些只想变得富有和成功、却不追求幸福或开悟的人，是还不够贪。你知道，也许他们该再自私一点、再贪一点。而那会很了不起。一种了不起的人生，是在每一个维度上都拿到七成、八成——而不是在一个维度上冲到一百五、其他维度全是零。每一维度都过线就行。我不觉得这是巧合：佛陀的故事里，佛陀是王子；你读《瑜伽·伐西斯塔》《阿什塔瓦克拉歌》里那些古老的开悟故事，开悟的总是国王或王子，很少是农民——他们够聪明，知道穷人得下地干活，得先想明白怎么养活自己，没有那份闲工夫坐着沉思「我是谁」。所以传统里才有两条路：要么你一开始就舍弃一切，去做苦行者，然后专心追求心智的自由；要么你从王子做起，最后意识到这一切都毫无价值。

Naval: So that's an easier path. I guess I want to find happiness and spiritual enlightenment and all those things, but I'm not willing to give up my world for it. I'm a greedy one, right? I might be the greediest person alive 'cause I want to be, you know, I want to be Elon. I want to be a successful entrepreneur. I want to be Thiel. I want to be a successful investor. I want a great household life. I want to be healthy as long as I can. I don't mind being famous, you know. But I also want enlightenment. I'm not willing to give that up. So to me, you know, the people who are aspiring to just be wealthy and successful but not happy or enlightened, they're not greedy enough. You know, maybe they need to be a little more selfish, a little more greedy. And you know, it'd be amazing. An amazing life is 70, 80% in each of those categories without going 150 on one and zero in any other. You get above the threshold on each of them. I don't think it's a coincidence that the Buddha story, you know, Buddha was a prince, or if you read all the old enlightenment stories in Yoga Vasistha, the Ashtavakra Gita, it's always the king or the prince who's becoming enlightened. It's rarely like the peasant, 'cause they were smart enough to realize the poor man has to, you know, dig the fields. He has to figure out how to feed himself. He doesn't have the luxury of sitting around and contemplating and saying who am I? So that's why in the tradition either you're someone who gives up everything right at the beginning, you go become an ascetic, and then you can like focus on mental freedom, or you start as a prince and you realize it's all worthless.

埃里克：难的应该是那个吧——既要有能量、干劲和动力先成为王子，又愿意把这一切都放下。我是说，那是一趟了不起的英雄之旅，尤其你列了这么多：想当埃隆·马斯克，想当蒂尔，想当伟大的创始人、伟大的投资人。

Eric: The hard one I guess would be having the energy and drive and motivation to become a prince and then be willing to give it all up. I mean, that's a big hero's journey as you list all those things that, you know, you want to be Elon Musk, you want to be Thiel, or you want to be a great founder, a great investor.

纳瓦尔：对。我得在这儿把预期调低一点，因为你知道，我老了，没有二十岁年轻人的那股精力，所以确实得相应地把欲望收在一个范围内。

Naval: Yeah, I have to temper my expectations here 'cause I'm, you know, I'm old, so I don't have the energy level of the 20-year-olds, so I do have to bound my desires accordingly.

埃里克：不过你已经实现了其中不少。我好奇的是，你知道，你能不能用一种很温和的方式握住这些欲望，让它们不伤到你？你有没有找到某种平衡——仍在努力，仍在期待，却又不……？

Eric: Well, you've achieved a lot of them already. And I guess I'm curious, you know, can you hold those desires in like a very gentle way where they don't harm you? Have you found a balance with, you know, still striving, still hoping but not?

纳瓦尔：对。不，我觉得可以。我觉得这肯定比那些在做心理治疗、在吸毒、精神崩溃或者总觉得自己被攻击的人要轻松得多。但我也曾经是那种人。一部分大概只是年纪大了；但很大一部分是内省、自我反思，还有——你知道，就是意识到这一切到头来都会归零。那句话怎么说的来着——裹尸布上没有口袋，对吧？你什么也带不走。这一切只是一场盛大的冒险。我有个朋友说得好，他管这叫「终局内容」。你知道，我不知道你玩没玩过马里奥，像《超级马里奥：奥德赛》、马里奥 3D 或者马里奥 64 什么的。你在那些游戏里一路闯关，整场都在追桃子公主和酷霸王，最后打完了。然后他们给你一个关卡，里头全是金币、蘑菇仔和蘑菇，你就跑来跑去、跳来跳去、玩得挺高兴——它好玩，但赌注很低，对吧？就是图个乐子。那是终局内容，你可以玩上一阵。我大概就处在这种终局内容里：到了这个点上，我没什么可输的。好，我可能会亏点钱，丢一点名声，我不在乎。我愿意当众摔个嘴啃泥，但我能不带着恐惧去玩——这意味着我可以去追最高的目标。它的缺点是，你知道，一个好的风投可能会说：你说你毫无恐惧地玩，是什么意思？我要你熬通宵，我要你怕，我要你拼命干。但我不确定那是做成一件事的唯一办法。我不觉得史蒂夫·乔布斯是从恐惧出发的，我甚至觉得埃隆也不是，对吧？所以我觉得从激励出发更好。这才让我能去尝试做一件真正困难、也真正值得的事。

Naval: Yeah. No, I think you can. I think it can definitely be easier than all the people who are like in therapy or doing drugs or mental wrecks or feel attacked. But I was that person too. I think some of it is just aging. But a lot of it is just introspection and self-reflection and you know just realizing that this all goes to zero anyway. You know what is the line—shrouds have no pockets, right? So, you don't take anything with you. It is all just a grand adventure. A friend of mine has a good phrase that he uses where he says endgame content. You know, it's like I don't know if you played the Mario games like Super Mario Odyssey or Mario 3D or Mario 64, any of those. And in those like you play the game, you run through all the levels. You know, you chase Peach and Bowser the whole game. And at the end, you're done. And then they give you like a level where it's just like all gold and Toads and mushrooms and you just run around and you jump and you play and you know you're having—it's fun but the stakes are very low, right? This is all entertaining. It's endgame content and you can play it for a little while. So, I kind of feel like I'm in endgame content where it's like I got nothing to lose at this point. Okay, I might lose some money, you know, a little reputation. I don't care about that. I'm fine falling on my face in public, but I get to play without fear and that means that I can go for the highest goals. So the downside to that is, you know, a good VC might be like, what do you mean you're playing without that fear? I want you up all night. You know, I want you fearful. I want you working hard. But I'm not sure that's the only way to build something. I don't think Steve Jobs was coming from a place of fear. I don't even think Elon was coming from a place of fear, right? So I think it's better to operate out of inspiration. And this means that I can try to do something that's really difficult and it's really worthwhile.

埃里克：对。也许有些真正宏大的项目，只有从这种状态里才做得成。

Eric: Yeah. There may be that some of those really aspirational projects can only be done from a place like that.

纳瓦尔：我觉得是。我不认为这是巧合：苹果、特斯拉、SpaceX——我是说，这些公司都是自筹资金的。大多数伟大的公司，要么自筹资金，要么在真正需要风投之前很早就跑出来了。谷歌就是这样。他们拿风投只是为了把约翰·杜尔和迈克·莫里茨请进董事会，可他们早就到了逃逸速度，成了。微软也是。他们从一位投资人那儿拿了一笔很小的钱，但早就一路狂奔了。同样，你知道，我记得亚马逊很快就盈利了。他们只拿了一小笔投资，但这些巨头里没有一家是靠一轮接一轮接一轮、按标准路径苦熬出来的。真正最大的那批公司没走过那条路。要么——或者说 Facebook 盈利得非常非常快，对吧？靠的是网络效应。所以要么它们迅速盈利、几乎不需要风投，要么它们是由一位有远见的创业者自筹起来的。而那种巨大的、标志性的伟大公司，靠一轮又一轮风投建起来的，极其罕见。

Naval: I think so. I don't think it's a coincidence that like Apple and Tesla and SpaceX, I mean, these are self-financed companies. Most of the great companies are either self-financed or they break out very early before they really need venture capital. So, Google broke out. They raised VC just to get John Doerr and Mike Moritz on the board, but they were already at escape velocity and they were done. Microsoft as well. They took a tiny check from one investor, but they were already off to the races. Same way with, you know, I think Amazon was profitable very quickly. They take a small investment, but none of these giant companies did like round after round after round grinding it out through the standard path. The truly largest companies did not do that. Either—or Facebook got profitable very, very fast, right? Because of the network effect. So either they got profitable very quickly and they almost didn't need the venture capital or they were self-financed by a visionary entrepreneur. But it's very rare that one of the huge iconic great companies was built by round after round after round of venture capital.

埃里克：这是个异端。

Eric: That's a heresy.

纳瓦尔：这话对。对，对。我对投资人没多少敬意，但我也不是不尊重他们。我觉得他们是生态里重要的一部分，你知道，我也是个资本主义者；只是我觉得他们不必那么自我表扬。

Naval: There you go. Yeah. Yeah. I don't have a lot of respect for investors. I don't disrespect them. I think they're an important part of the ecosystem and you know I'm a capitalist, but I don't think they need to pat themselves on the back.

埃里克：你觉得有些想要的东西彼此互斥吗？

Eric: Do you think wanting some things are mutually incompatible?

纳瓦尔：我是说，它们必须互斥，对吧？对。嗯，你又回到了那个——你知道，智力唯一真正的检验，是你有没有从人生里得到自己想要的。就像《圣经》里那句：骆驼穿过针眼，比富人进天国还容易。我的理解是：如果你在追物质的东西，就没法同时追精神的东西，对吧？你不可能既在物质生活里成功、为之奋力奔波、做个商人，同时又成为佛陀。所以它们也许是互相矛盾的。

Naval: I mean, they have to be, right? Yeah. Well, you back to the, you know, the only true test of intelligence is whether you get what you want out of life. Like, I mean, the biblical phrase like easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to get into heaven. What I interpret that as is like if you're pursuing material things, you can't pursue spiritual things, right? You can't both be successful in this material life and striving for it and living the life of a merchant then also be a Buddha. So they might be mutually contradictory.

我想有天晚上我熬得很晚，一边拉伸一边想：伙计，我得确保拉伸永远是我日常的一部分。然后我又想：我的日常到底是什么？我在这儿到底想干什么？于是我就把它打了出来，发成一条推文。结果那是我得到过的最高评价之一——我想是这位叫 mmay3r 的人说：哦，你把那本书写出来了。

I think I was up late one night and I was stretching and I was like man I should just make sure stretching is always part of my routine. I was like what is my routine? What am I trying to do here? So then I just typed that out and then I tweeted that out and I got one of the highest compliments I got on a tweet, which is this guy I think mmay3r, he said, oh you wrote the book.

埃里克：对。你用这一条，把一整本书塞进了一条推文里。我看到那么多人照着做，所以它显然挺能引起共鸣。

Eric: Yeah. You put a book in a tweet with this one. And I've seen so many people replicate this, so it's clearly kind of resonant.

纳瓦尔：对，不过里头有个错误，挺糟的。有一样东西我最后会换掉。

Naval: Yeah, there was a mistake in there, though, which kind of sucks. There was one thing that I'll swap out at the end.

埃里克：好，我念到那儿的时候你纠正我。「禁食，举重，冲刺，拉伸，冥想。」

Eric: All right, correct me when I get here. "Fast, lift, sprint, stretch, and meditate."

纳瓦尔：等等。禁食，举重，冲刺，拉伸，冥想。所以这些全是身体层面的。

Naval: So, hold on. Fast, lift, sprint, stretch, meditate. So, that's all the physical stuff.

埃里克：对。是的，差不多是晨间日常、每日锻炼那一套。我喜欢它排在第一位，因为你把健康放在了最优先。

Eric: Yep. Yeah. Kind of the morning routines, you know, daily workout thing. And I like that it's first 'cause you're starting with health as your first priority.

纳瓦尔：没错。

Naval: That's right.

埃里克：「建造，出售，写作，创造，投资，拥有。」

Eric: "Build, sell, write, create, invest, and own."

纳瓦尔：对。这一段是赚钱、财富的部分。对。把自己产品化。去建造一家公司，或者买下它的股权。

Naval: Yeah. So, this is the making-money, the wealth part. Yeah. Productize yourself. Build or buy equity in a business.

埃里克：「阅读，反思，去爱，寻求真理，无视社会。」

Eric: "Read, reflect, love, seek truth, and ignore society."

纳瓦尔：真理和爱，再配上恰到好处的一份自私。

Naval: Yeah. Habits. 'Cause that's the only way you stick with them.
